--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- Champion: Single state GoAwayColor(green=2) → score -11.61. This makes sense! Green is the BAD side (increases score), so robots run away from green walls, which drives them toward the BLUE side and nest area. Actually wait - GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green) means robots go away from green, which pushes them toward blue. But they need to pick up items from blue and deliver to nest. The single-state approach works surprisingly well.
- Large gap between rank 1 (-11.61) and rank 2 (-2.66).
- The key insight: clr=2 is GREEN (the bad half). Going away from green walls → robots end up near blue source or nest area.

**Color mapping:** 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Strategy:**
- The champion goes away from GREEN. This likely drives robots to the BLUE side source and then to nest.
- We need robots to: go to blue source, pick up item, go to nest (white floor, red wall), deliver.
- GoToColor(blue=3) could bring robots to blue source. GoAwayColor(green=2) avoids the bad source.
- GoToColor(red=1) could bring robots to nest wall (nest side).

**Plan:**
1. **SURVIVAL**: Copy champion exactly.
2. **EXPLOITATION**: Variations of the champion - try different color targets, slight modifications.
3. **EXPLORATION**: Multi-state FSMs that explicitly navigate: explore → go to blue source → go to red wall (nest).

Key FSM ideas:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green), transition on white floor (in nest) → stay, on black floor (source) → something
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3) to find blue source, on black floor → GoToColor(red=1) to return to nest
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2), on white floor → exploration, on black floor → GoToColor(red=1)

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion copy: `--nstates 1 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 4 --clr0 2`
2. Exploit: GoAwayColor(green) with transitions - on white floor go explore, on black go to red wall
3. Exploit: Two states - GoAwayColor(green) + GoToColor(red=1) - when on black floor (picked up item) go to red wall
4. Exploit: GoToColor(blue=3) single state
5. Exploit: Two states - GoToColor(blue) + GoToColor(red) switching on floor color
6. Three states: explore → GoToColor(blue) → GoToColor(red)
7. Two states: GoAwayColor(green) → GoToColor(red) on black floor
8. Exploit champion with different clr or vel
9. Three states with floor color transitions
10-14: Various explorations

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2:**

The champion (-13.22) is a 2-state FSM:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2) with 3 transitions: on BLACK floor→stay(p=0.8), perceive blue(3)→stay(p=0.7), on WHITE floor→stay(p=0.2). Wait - destination 0 from state 0 would mean staying in state 0... Actually `--n0x0 0` with 2 states means D=0 which targets state 1 (the only other state). Let me re-read: "destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0."

So champion state 0: GoAwayColor(green), transitions all go to state 1 (GoToColor red=1):
- On BLACK floor (p=0.8) → GoToColor(red=1)  
- Perceive blue (p=0.7) → GoToColor(red=1)
- On WHITE floor (p=0.2) → GoToColor(red=1)

State 1: GoToColor(red=1), transitions back to state 0:
- On WHITE floor (p=0.9) → GoAwayColor(green)
- On GRAY floor (p=0.4) → GoAwayColor(green) (wait, c=1 is GRAY)

This makes behavioral sense: robots go away from green (toward blue source), when they detect black floor or blue walls they've arrived at source → go toward red wall (nest). When on white floor (delivered) → go away from green again.

**Key patterns that work:**
- GoAwayColor(green=2) as main foraging behavior
- GoToColor(red=1) as return-to-nest behavior
- Transitions: BLACK floor or perceive BLUE → switch to return; WHITE floor → switch to forage

**Strategy for Round 3:**
1. Survive champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary probabilities of champion transitions (p=0.8→0.9, p=0.7→0.8/0.6, p=0.2→0.1/0.3)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The top machines are clearly converging on a 2-state topology:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2), transitions → state 1 on: BLACK floor (p~0.8-0.9), perceive BLUE/3 (p~0.7-0.8), WHITE floor (p~0.1-0.3)
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1), transitions → state 0 on: WHITE floor (p~0.9-0.95), GRAY floor (p~0.3-0.5)

Key observations:
- Best: p_black=0.85, p_blue=0.75, p_white=0.1 in state 0; p_white=0.9, p_gray=0.5 in state 1
- Second best: p_black=0.9, p_blue=0.8, p_white=0.15; p_white=0.95, p_gray=0.35
- The WHITE floor transition from state 0 seems to hurt slightly (robot returns when delivered, but should keep foraging) - keeping it low (0.1-0.15) is best
- Adding a 3rd transition perceiving RED (c=7, l=1) in state 1 helped (#4 score -12.80)
- 3-state FSMs with exploration state hurt performance

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Survive champion
2. Fine-tune champion: vary p_black in [0.8, 0.9, 0.95], p_blue in [0.7, 0.8, 0.85], p_white in [0.05, 0.1, 0.15]
3. Vary state 1 transitions: p_white in [0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0], p_gray in [0.3, 0.4, 0.5, 0.6]
4. Try adding perceive-RED transition in state 1
5. Try removing white-floor transition from state 0 entirely (just black + blue)
6. Try higher p_blue (perceiving blue walls is very reliable signal for being at source)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

Very tight clustering - all machines score between -12.90 and -13.79. The champion has only 2 transitions in state 0 (no white floor transition), which is interesting - it dropped the white-floor condition and still won.

Key observations:
- Champion: state 0 has 2 transitions: BLACK(p=0.85) and perceive-BLUE(p=0.75). No white floor transition.
- State 1 unchanged: WHITE(p=0.9), GRAY(p=0.5)
- The white floor transition from state 0 seems slightly harmful (keeps sending robots back when they're already at nest delivering)
- Scores are very close (-12.9 to -13.79), suggesting we're near a local optimum
- All the top machines use the same 2-state topology with GoAwayColor(green) → GoToColor(red)

**What to try:**
1. Survive champion
2. Fine-tune champion parameters: vary p_black (0.75-0.95), p_blue (0.65-0.85)
3. Fine-tune state 1: vary p_white (0.85-0.95), p_gray (0.4-0.6)
4. Try removing the blue-perception transition (just black floor)
5. Try adding perceive-GREEN transition in state 0 (going away from green, if you perceive green that's another signal)
6. Try perceive-RED in state 1 (more sensitive detection of nest)
7. Explore: try GoToColor(blue) as state 0 then GoToColor(red) as state 1 with black floor trigger
8. Try different probability combinations systematically

The champion's simplicity (2 transitions in state 0) suggests less is more. Let me focus on finding the best probability values.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

The champion (-14.24) persists from round 5. This round's best was -13.86, not beating the champion. We seem to be in a very tight local optimum around -13 to -14.

Key observations:
- The champion: p_black=0.85, p_blue=0.75, p_white=0.95, p_gray=0.45
- Round 6 best: p_blue=0.70 (lower than champion's 0.75) scored -13.86
- p_white=1.0 (rank 8: -13.26) - not better than 0.95
- p_gray=0.4 (rank 13: -12.79) - worse than 0.45
- p_gray=0.35 (rank 3: -13.45) - slightly worse than 0.45
- p_gray=0.5 (rank 2: -13.50) - slightly worse than 0.45

The champion parameters seem well-tuned. The problem is stochasticity - scores vary by ~1 between runs. We need to either:
1. Keep re-sampling near champion to confirm it's best
2. Try genuinely different approaches to escape

**What hasn't been tried much:**
- Different colors: what if BLUE walls are the bad ones? Let me reconsider: GoAwayColor(green=2) drives robots away from green. If blue decreases score (good), then we want robots near blue source. GoAwayColor(green) could work by avoiding the green (bad) side.
- Try GoToColor(blue=3) to explicitly seek blue source, then GoToColor(red=1) to return
- Try varying p_black_s0 more widely (0.7, 0.75)
- Try p_blue_s0 at 0.65, 0.6 (lower)
- The round 6 #1 had p_blue=0.70 with score -13.86 - not quite champion but close

For exploration, let me try:
- Swapping which color in state 0 is the "go away" target (try clr=1 for red instead)
- Different p_gray values in state 1 around 0.45
- Try adding a very small probability random transition

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (-14.24) still holds from round 5. This round's best was -13.98, still not beating it. We're clearly in a tight local optimum.

Key observations:
- Rank 1 (-13.98): Added BLACK floor transition (p=0.8) to state 1 → slightly better than pure champion but not beating it
- Rank 2 (-13.93): Added second BLACK floor transition (p=0.85) to state 0 → interesting, redundant but competitive
- Rank 13 (-5.19): Replacing GRAY→state0 with BLACK→state0 in state 1 was catastrophic
- p_blue=0.65 and p_blue=0.72 both worse → 0.75 seems right
- p_black=0.83 worse, p_black=0.87 slightly worse → 0.85 is near optimal
- p_white=0.93-0.97 all similar → 0.95 is fine
- p_gray=0.42-0.48 all near 0.45 → 0.45 is fine

**What to try:**
The stochasticity is high (~1 unit variance). The champion score of -14.24 might be partly luck. I should:
1. Keep champion (required)
2. Re-run champion-like machines to get better statistics
3. Try some genuinely different topologies to escape the local optimum

**New exploration ideas:**
- What if we use GoToColor(blue=3) in state 0 to actively seek blue source, then GoToColor(red=1) to return? This is more explicit.
- What if state 1 uses GoAwayColor(green=2) instead of GoToColor(red=1)? Both push toward nest from source side.
- Try a version where the second transition in state 0 detects BLACK floor (not blue color)
- Try p_black=0.85 only (no blue perception) with higher p to compensate
- Try very different p combinations: p_black=0.7, p_blue=0.9

Given the variance, I'll include several re-runs of near-champion configurations plus genuine exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

The champion (-14.24) still holds. Round 8's best was -14.21, very close. The scores are clustering tightly around -13 to -14.

Key observations:
- Rank 1 (-14.21): Added BLACK floor (p=0.75) transition to state 1 → nearly champion-level
- Rank 2 (-13.95): p_gray=0.5 in state 1 (vs 0.45 in champion) → slightly worse
- Rank 3 (-13.93): Added perceive-RED (p=0.8) to state 1 → competitive
- The core structure is extremely stable: GoAwayColor(green) → GoToColor(red)
- Adding BLACK floor trigger to state 1 at p=0.75 seems promising (rank 1 result)

**Pattern emerging:** Adding a BLACK floor transition to state 1 (GoToColor red) at moderate probability seems to help. When robots are in state 1 (heading to nest) but land on black floor somehow (perhaps overshooting), the BLACK condition in state 1 fires and sends them back to state 0. Wait - that would be bad. Actually if they're in state 1 and on black floor, that means they're still at source - so transitioning back to state 0 (GoAwayColor green) makes sense.

Actually the rank 1 machine has in state 1: WHITE(p=0.95)→s0, GRAY(p=0.45)→s0, BLACK(p=0.75)→s0. When on black floor in state 1, they switch to GoAwayColor(green) which is correct - they're still at source!

**Plan for Round 9:**
1. Survive champion
2. Focus exploitation on the 3-transition state 1 variant (white + gray + black)
3. Try different p_black values in state 1: 0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85
4. Try combining with slight variations of state 0
5. Keep some re-runs of the champion structure
6. A little exploration with different structures

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
